# Agreements, draft 26: the companion

*Beside `draft-26.md`, not part of it, and not hashed. Sections 1 to 6 by the scribe's session M9, 27 November 2026; sections 7 to 11 and "What draft 26 hands to other texts" by M10. Sections 6.11 and 6.12, and the few edits listed in the trace under "M11", by M11, the whole check. Section 6.13, scenarios 8.22 to 8.26, the wave-four entries of section 5, and the edits listed in the trace under "N1", by N1, the scribe's session of wave four. Sections 6.14 to 6.16, scenarios 8.27 to 8.36, the wave-five entries of section 5, the table of the 26 rules that moved, and the edits listed in the trace under "P2", by P2, the scribe's second session of wave five, on draft 26 as the scribe's session P1 left it; every quotation of a rule P1 changed is brought to its words as they now stand. It explains draft 26 and changes none of its words: where a reason can be read two ways, both are given; where his words and the MOR lead's line differ, both are quoted and each is labelled. It adds no rule, settles nothing left out, and proposes no remedy. Nothing here is a grammar rule: a rule found only here would be no rule.*

**The text it explains.** `draft-26.md` as N1 left it on branch `claude/draft-26-outline-vbtvu7`: `SPEC_DRAFT_26` = `04bae25cb3416e111e1f65ddca2c99c5769d3945c0751a6846621929c0d29904` (encoding 3, E2: the core library's `Spec::mir_file_hash`); sha256 of the file's bytes (`sha256sum draft-26.md`) `09ba9ce52f1e995872aaf7a35838442516f7c014e0b104256f273c2c7f0f85ff`. Not merged, and not LATEST.

**The wish** (Nobody, allegedly, 26 November, 16:46, at the top of `docs/decisions.md` on main): "We need to change the current wish to updating the core after the findings". On what carries over (16:47): "Yes, we’ll go back to analysis, maybe add the focus on agreements". On earlier answers (26 November, 16:03): "Yes, but me saying something does not mean that something was necessarily a good idea".

**How to read it.** *The ledger* is `docs/decisions.md` on main; an exchange is cited by its bold heading and date. Each exchange ends in an "Arrived at (to check)" line: that line is the MOR lead's reading of the exchange, not his words, and is labelled *the lead's line* here. His words are quoted exactly, typos as the ledger has them. *The lead's reading* is a ruling the MOR lead gave the writing sessions on 27 November, not yet in the ledger; those of the evening are in `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-draft-26-readings.md` and `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-readings-for-p2.md` on main, every line the lead's (sections 6.14 to 6.16). *The scribe's reading* is this companion's own (the scribe being the session that writes draft 26 and this file). Words in bold or in draft 26's sense are defined in draft 26's §3. Draft 26's rules are cited by their numbers (5.5.1); draft 25's by its own (G4.8), since the reasons were first given under those. *The brief* is `draft-26-brief-2.md`, *the trace* `draft-26-trace.md`, *the outline* `draft-26-outline.md`, all beside this file. *The settled note* is `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-settled-by-the-lead.md` on main: every row of it is the lead's reading.

## 1. The spine: "What is the minimum two people have to write"

**In plain words.** Before draft 26 there was a question, put to people who had never heard of MOR: what does an agreement need? Their answer was a list of small tasks and a handful of operations that answer them. Read beside MOR, the operations turned out to be MOR's other layers, and what the operations could not do turned out to be the sockets where people plug in their own rules. Agreements is what is left once those are taken away: a short grammar for reading who signed what, in what order.

He asked for the spine on 17 November ("A companion for Agreements"):

> "Agreements has become quite abstract. It will need a good companion that explains why it is structured as such and how we got that that shape."
>
> "The how we got there needs to build around the research we ran on agreeing. « What is the minimum two people have to write »"
>
> "It's also a good place to state that what the review found was MOR other layers and the sockets."

The lead's line: "the companion's story of how Agreements got its shape is built around the research on how people agree, with his question as its spine: what is the minimum two people have to write"; and "the companion states that what the review of how people agree found turned out to be MOR's other layers and the sockets."

### 1.1 The research

On 10 November he asked windows that knew nothing of MOR to work out, from nothing, what agreeing requires. The questions used no MOR word, and not the word "grammar". Their texts are kept as received in `docs/strangers-2026-11/` on main (`README.md`).

**The minimum, answered.** `agreement-tasks.md` starts from four points: a record is "a defense against the other party's future self, and one's own"; "Each party must hold the other's commitment, not their own"; a mark deters a lie only if an outsider could be shown it, "So every written agreement is written for a third person, even one who never turns up"; and so: "the least two people need to write down is exactly the least a third person needs to see." That is the answer to his question, and it is the whole shape of draft 26 in one line: the grammar rules are what a third person, the verifier, can see from signed acts alone (draft 26 §1, "One constraint on everything below"; §3.6, Verifier).

**Forty-nine tasks.** The same file reasons through ten situations, asking each time "what new lie or failure becomes possible that the existing tasks do not defend against?", and arrives at 49 tasks in seven groups (making it, who holds what, doing it, acting through others, staying true over time, going wrong, ending). Draft 25 asked the same question of each of its own rules, in its "two questions" sentence (section 5 below).

**Five operations.** `agreement-grammar.md` tests the 49 against five operations a record can perform: **State** ("fix a set of words so they cannot shift"), **Sign**, **Deliver**, **Order** ("place them in a shared sequence, so "before", "after" and "latest" have an answer") and **Control** ("have power over a thing, so it moves only when a condition is met"); with a silent sixth, **Apply** ("applying a stated rule to known inputs", which "needs no trust: anyone can redo the calculation"). 33 of the 49 come out clean. **Keep** and **Check** come out as qualities, not tasks: a result must last, and an outsider must be able to test it. Draft 26 names the five operations in its §1 ("it names those operations (State, Sign, Deliver, Order, Control) where it needs them") and carries Keep and Check in its last sentence there ("What an act fixes must still be there and unaltered when needed, and an outsider holding copies must be able to test it"); draft 25 cited them by the research's numbers (#9 Keep, #10 Check), which draft 26 leaves out as history (the trace, §1).

**What no record reaches.** The residue is three things: **truth** ("Whenever the agreement touches the world outside the record, someone must say what happened, and their signature proves only that they said it"), **minds** (what someone understood, wanted or knows), and **hands** ("Words can order a release; something must obey"). For minds the file says: "do not try to prove; assign in advance who bears the failure", and its task list adds that in open offers "the whole burden of Align falls on the one who writes the words". Draft 26's 5.3.2 is that sentence in the grammar: "The authors are the parties who bear the failure of the words to mean one thing" (draft 25 tagged it "#4 Align").

**Four outside roles.** What the parties cannot do alone sorts into "A register: one ordered place to look", "A witness of the world", "A hand: has power over the value", and "A judge: decides when the others are disputed". Absences ("nothing later exists", "this is all of it") "can be solved, but only by a single place to look".

**No single shared sequence.** The second test supposes each party keeps only its own chained sequence. Twelve tasks fall, in two clusters, "only once" and "by when"; what survives is "two people signing words to each other". Four findings there are draft 26's ground for order: a shared sequence is needed "for exactly two things: scarcity (this thing, to one person only) and freshness (nothing later exists; the deadline has passed)"; "A fork convicts itself. Two signed entries pointing at the same predecessor prove the signer lied, with no judge"; a witness need only say "I saw this entry at this point in my own sequence"; and the closed world becomes local: "A thing's ownership is exactly as global as its witness set." And one sentence there is draft 26's principle before it had a name: "An amendment needs both signatures, so neither party can be surprised by one. Only a stranger arriving later is exposed." (Section 3.)

**The record cannot hold value.** The third test supposes the record holds only statements about value held elsewhere, which is MOR's situation for money. "The record falls back from control to proof"; the holder becomes an agent, acting for another; and "The receiver's signed receipt becomes the only clean proof that value moved." Draft 26's buyer's confirmation (5.11.7) and leg reports (5.11.3) are that receipt.

**Roles filled later.** `late-roles.md` (12 November) found about eighteen roles that agreements have always named before knowing who would fill them ("whoever brings a buyer", "whoever holds this ticket", "my heirs"), and that they "link to a person in only four ways: by doing something, by receiving from the previous holder, by holding an object, or by being someone who fits a description." Draft 26's four named kinds of spot are those four: **done**, **received**, **holding**, **fits**, with an open fifth, **future** (5.10.1). The person who fills such a spot is the unknown party of section 3. (The scribe's reading: the file is the research named in the assignment; no ledger line ties the four kinds to it.)

### 1.2 What the review found

Fable read the strangers' texts the same evening, for the MOR lead (`docs/reviews/fable-after-the-task-list.md`, on main; Fable's reading throughout, labelled so in that file). Two lines of it are what he meant by "what the review found":

- "**The operations are the layers.** State is Text, sign is Identity, deliver and order are Envelopes, control is what Money records, apply is the verifier. Agreements has no operation of its own. It is the list of what must be checked when the others are combined."
- "**Three kinds of socket.** Every place where the core hands something to a cMIP is one of: trust this witness, apply this rule, trust this hand. What depends on a mind is a stated cost."

So the strangers, starting from nothing, found MOR's other layers (the five operations) and its sockets (the four outside roles, three of which plug in as modules of a cMIP: "a rule that computes, a witness, a judge, a teller and the like", draft 26 §3.1, Module; the fourth, the register, became the keeper, §5.8). What was left for Agreements was the reading: who is bound by which Terms, and what is shown, unknown or contradicted (draft 26 §1). That is why draft 26 defines no operation of its own and names the layers it reads (§2), and why every place where people choose how they agree is a socket (§3.1, Task; §6).

The same review gave the test draft 25 wrote into its §1, quoted in section 5 below: "First: is there a lie here that signed, ordered text cannot already expose? If not, it is only text. Second: does catching it hurt someone who did not choose the rule? If not, it is a task for a cMIP."

**Round two, read the same way** (the scribe's reading). The simulation round of November ran on draft 25 and ended in the findings this stint answers ("updating the core after the findings"). `docs/research/round-2-what-the-language-could-not-say.md` (branch `claude/research-round-2-could-not-say`, pull request 232) counted what the windows could not say in the language. Its three most raised labels are the strangers' residue again: "hours and "by when" came from Postmark's place and Longhouse's anchors, never from the acts" (17 of 25 windows); "no act says "nothing else", "not delivered", "this is all of it"" (13); "a ladder, a photo, an arbiter's independence: only someone's word" (9). Draft 26's answers to the first two are the keeper and the clock the Terms name (§5.8) and "Missing act is always "I don’t know"" (§5.13); the third stays a witness in a socket. Whether this is what he meant by "the review" is not said: his line of 17 November was said before round two.

## 2. The evidence-and-accounting line

**In plain words.** The strangers' research ended in one sentence about what a record is when it cannot move money and has no single clock. He wants it beside MOR's own opening definition. That definition is the MICS's, so the sentence is placed there when the MICS is rewritten; here it is quoted as the record has it.

The close of `docs/strangers-2026-11/agreement-grammar.md` ("Both suppositions together"):

> "Title, Transfer and Sum lose on both counts. What stays clean is bilateral: signed words, receipts and calculation. The record becomes an evidence and accounting layer: it proves who owes what and who lied, and it moves nothing. Its only force is what a proven liar stands to lose."

The exchange, "An evidence and accounting layer" (17 November): asked for "an interesting definition of MOR" a window gave at its end, the MOR lead fetched that close; he quoted it back, then:

> "An evidence and accounting layer…. That bit has to complement the opening definition of MOR"

The lead's line: "the stranger's sentence complements the opening definition of MOR (today the MICS's "The test": "MOR is the law where bytes can prove it, and keeps the record where they cannot"), to be placed when the MICS is rewritten."

So the MICS places it, when it is next written (the settled note's M24: the MICS is rewritten after draft 26). This companion does not word it beside the definition: that is the MICS's to do.

How it reads in Agreements (the scribe's reading): draft 26 moves nothing. A payment is the Money layer's act (§3.1, Payment); the settlement is that layer's evidence (5.9.1); a leg report and the buyer's confirmation are each one side's signed word (5.11.3, 5.11.7). What a verifier gives is who is bound, who signed what, and where two signed words disagree: "it proves who owes what and who lied". A keeper that lies about order "signs the lie" (the lead's words in "The place vouches for the chaining only", 26 November); a signer who forks their own line convicts themselves (5.7.2). What a proven liar stands to lose is not this text's.

---

## 3. The principle: protect the unknown party

**In plain words.** Some people join an agreement after it was made: a buyer who takes up an offer, anyone who fills an open spot. They were not there when the Terms were written and have no say in how they change. Draft 26 protects them in one way: whatever they joined, they keep, as it was when they joined, for as long as its own words say. No later change by the people who made the agreement reaches them. That is the principle. It is stated here, not in draft 26; draft 26's rules apply it.

### 3.1 The exchanges

The principle was named twice in one quarter of an hour on 26 November, in two exchanges that ran side by side, and placed a minute later.

**"Whose place counts; protect the unknown party, as a principle"** (26 November, 15:51–16:04). The MOR lead asked whether a place (draft 25's word for what draft 26 calls a keeper: section 4.6) named only by a rule the parties never signed may order their Terms, and leaned no, not as the Terms' own answer. He:

> "I think you are right. It is worth turning it into a principle. Protect the unknown party"

The lead's lines: "a place named only by a rule the parties never signed does not order their Terms. A verifier may give an answer per such a place only as the reader's choice, named so"; and ""Protect the unknown party" is a principle of MOR. Where it is written is not yet asked."

**"A buyer under a replaced version: what version 2 says"** (26 November, 15:49–16:03). Ana bought by her assent to version 2; version 3 replaced it. He (15:52):

> "Yes, and the what she bought creates different scenarios probably addressed differently under different cMIPs. We’re talking about the right to update terms on an ongoing agreement. Ana is a different class of participant, she is unknown at the time of creation of the agreement. Therefore, she has no say in the updating of it, only the known parties can. She should be protected. Version 2 is what she bought, and she has no say in the update. Let”s look at a service… it would protect her from terms being changed on her. If she bought for a month of service to an LLM for example, the deal is valid a month. If it was year, version 2 is valid a year. Protect the unknown parties."

Asked whether it holds where version 2's own update condition lets the seller change terms for existing buyers (draft 25's G10.6(b)), he:

> "Yes, it was a bad idea. It’s the “let me drown you in terms updatable unilaterally” nightmare we already live under"

The lead's line: "G10.6(b) goes. No later version changes the rights of a filling already earned except by an assent its holder signs in their own name. An update condition cannot reach what an unknown party bought."

**"Protect the unknown party is an Agreements principle"** (26 November, 16:05). Asked where it is written, the MOR lead leaned the working rules and the companion. He:

> "It’s an agreements principle"

The lead's line: "it belongs to Agreements: stated in the Agreements companion, applied by Agreements' rules."

**Then, how far it reaches.** "Who signs an update by default: the known parties" (26 November, 17:51–17:59): draft 25's default asked every filler to sign each update. He:

> "It’s the protect the unknown party expressed the old way."

and (17:59): "Yes, the difference is how the party is protected. They can chose not to renew under new terms, but whatever they paid for is covered by what they signed, not how it got updated. This creates a situation where two version of the agreement are valid though, depending on the party."

"For the unknown party there is no update, only the agreement she signed" (27 November, 07:53–07:55): the MOR lead asked whether the protection reaches only her rights or her whole filling. He: "Not sure I follow who “her “ is sorry", then:

> "There is no valid update for her anymore. Only a valid agreement, this is because she is the unknown party the network protects. No?"

The lead's line: "yes. For a filling, no later update exists: no update of the known parties reaches her in any way (her rights, her spot, her place in the agreement), because she is the unknown party the network protects. What exists for her is the agreement she signed, the version her proof names, which binds her and binds the seller to her for as long as its words say on the clock it names. The known parties may change the agreement for themselves and for newcomers, close the offer, or dissolve it among themselves; none of it touches her." His words end "No?"; the line reads them as yes (the brief, item 29).

"Ana's month ends by the clock the agreement names" (27 November, 07:50–07:53). He: "The clock specified in the agreement and its anchoring." Then:

> "The 17th is what lead to the 18th, we”re in a different “world now. We changed how the unknown party is protected. So, valid update does not apply in the same way. There is no valid update to sign, only signed terms to respect. It’s a problem that really only impacts subscriptions models I think"

### 3.2 The principle, stated

Protect the unknown party. A person who takes part in an agreement through a role (a spot filled after the Terms were made) is its **unknown party** (draft 26 §3.2). She has no say in the agreement's updates. What exists for her is the version her proof names: it binds her, by the assent with which she earned her filling, and it binds the known parties to her, for as long as its words say, counted on the clock that version names. No update of the known parties reaches her, in any way. Nothing changes what she earned except an assent she signs, herself or through a power she gave. That is no update reaching her: a later version changes what she earned only by her own signature on it, which is why 5.10.7 still asks a verifier to name "each later version that changes what it earned, with the assent that rests on" (the scribe's reading of 5.10.6 beside 5.10.7).

The old way and the new. Draft 25 protected her by a veto: its default asked every filler to sign every update (G4.8(a)), and its G10.6(b) still let her own version's update condition change her rights. Draft 26 protects her by reach: she signs no update, and no update reaches her. "It’s the protect the unknown party expressed the old way." The cost he names: "a problem that really only impacts subscriptions models I think" (M10's stated costs, section 7).

**Who she is.** Draft 26 says: "said of the holder of a filling: a signer who takes part through a role, and so was not known when the Terms that name that role were made. A spot that a known party of the version naming it fills is filled by a known party's act: that filling is no unknown party's, and the known parties' rules apply to it" (§3.2, Unknown party). Before wave five it said, on the settled note's M15 ("A filling is any filling of a role, whenever made"), "Any filling of a role is an unknown party's, whenever it was made". That clause went with his correction of 27 November, 18:46 ("“that fills an unknown party spot”", in "A filling gives no power"), whose line, the lead's, reads: "A spot a known party fills is a known party's act, and the known parties' rule applies to it"; and with the lead's reading F17/C10 (section 6.14). His earlier words are "she is unknown at the time of creation of the agreement". The brief's other reading (item 19), a person whose identity was known when the Terms were made and who still came in through a role, is not the case the correction names: one known to the drafters, and not named in the version as a party, still takes part through a role, and is an unknown party (the scribe's reading).

**Two classes.** He (18:47): "It’s seems important to really make a point that these two are not the same class or IDs and are not bound by the same rules." Draft 26 says so in §0 and at the head of §3.2 (**Two classes**), before any entry; the list of each class's rules there is the lead's line of 18:47, which his words do not give item by item (section 5, "Wave five"; section 6.16, the lead's reading 12 of 20:55).

**What the principle is, and is not** (the settled note's F16, the lead's reading): "G10.6 (a) is not an exception to "built on top, as a test": it is a guarantee no module may break (the admission test), not a rule about how people behave." "Built on top, as a test" (18 November) sends every rule "that settles how people should behave in a deal" to the cMIPs. The principle is kept in the grammar because no cMIP in any socket can be allowed to break it: whatever update condition the Terms name, it is "never asked about a filling" (5.5.7). Whether the principle binds a verifier beyond the rules that apply it is unsaid (the brief, item 16): the lead's line of 16:05 says "applied by Agreements' rules", and a verifier applies only grammar rules (§3.1, Grammar rule).

### 3.3 Where draft 26 applies it

**§5.10, the filling** (the principle itself):
- **5.10.6** "**No update reaches a filling.** For an unknown party's filling (§3.2), no later update exists"; "no power a version carries is used for its holder but one they gave in an act of their own (5.10.7)". Its holder has no say in updates; no update binds them; what exists for the holder is the version the filling's proof names; "A verifier MUST NOT read any update, or any update condition's answer, as reaching a filling"
- **5.10.7** **Rights.** "A filling has exactly the rights offered by the version it was earned under"; "Nothing changes what a filling earned except an assent its holder signs in their own name (5.6.1(a)), whether or not it is counted for the later version, or one made for them under a power they gave in an act of their own (5.6.1(b), 5.6.5(f)). An assent that fills an unknown party's spot gives no power: a power the version carries is given by its known parties and binds only them." The power clause is the lead's reading on G10.6(a) (section 6); "in an act of their own" and the sentence after it are "A filling gives no power" (27 November, 18:39–18:47) and the lead's sentence for draft 26 (section 5, "Wave five"); "counted or not" is the lead's reading F9/F10/C24 (section 6.14).
- **5.10.8** **How long, and no trigger.** What a filling gave "lasts as long as the words of the version it was earned under say, measured on the clock that version names, from the anchoring of the purchase"; the cMIP that reads those words answers; "No update ends what a filling gave, and none is needed" "the clock that version names", not the clock of the Terms in force, is the lead's reading of 13:20 (section 6).
- Beside them: 5.10.2 reads a filling already earned "from the version its proof names, its role and that role's cMIP as that version states them, whatever a later version says of the role" (the lead's reading of 13:20); 5.10.3 "what binds the known parties to the holder of a filling is the version its proof names"; 5.10.5 "a filling already earned stays a filling, shown with its proof".

**§5.6, who is bound:**
- **5.6.1(c)** binds a person by a Terms act they did not sign only "as a known party", and adds: "No update binds anyone through an unknown party's filling (§3.2): such a filling is bound by the version its proof names, and by no update (5.4.3, §5.10)"
- **5.6.2** "It binds no one through an unknown party's filling (§3.2): whoever holds one is bound by the version that filling's proof names, for as long as its words say, and by no update"
- **5.6.1(b)** and 5.1.2: a power to act for someone binds them only where their own signature is on the version that gives it, "the version `under` names", "and they are a known party of that version: no update condition can give a power for a person who did not sign the version that gives it, and an assent that fills an unknown party's spot gives no power; for a filling's holder, an act made under a power counts as their signature only where they gave that power in an act of their own (5.10.7)" ("Nothing about me without my signature", 18 November; "A filling gives no power", 27 November, 18:39–18:47). The MOR lead named this rule among those that carry the principle when he asked where it is written (16:05: "the core carrying it by the rules that apply it (G10.6, "nothing about me without my signature")").
- **5.6.6**, *Who is covered*: "a filling is governed by the version its proof names, which no update reaches"

**§5.5, updates:**
- **5.5.5(a)** and **5.5.7(e)**: the default signers of an update are the known parties, "never a party named by role, and never a signer who fills a role". She is not asked, because nothing she holds is at stake.
- **5.5.7**: the update condition "is never asked about a filling: no update reaches one (§5.10)"; 5.5.7(f): "What a filling gave lasts as long as the words of the version it was earned under say … no update ends it, and none is needed."
- **5.5.3**, with **5.8.5** and **5.13.5**: a keeper or an anchoring the Terms do not name, a reader's own or one named only by a rule the parties never signed, "gives an answer only as that reader's, named so: it never gives the Terms' own answer, never puts Terms in force, and never makes an update late". This is the exchange in which the principle was first named ("Whose place counts", 15:51). Who is protected there is anyone who relies on the Terms without having chosen the anchoring (the scribe's reading: the exchange names no person).

**What it protects her from** (the scribe's summary of the rules above): an update of the known parties changing what she bought, her spot or her place (5.10.6); the update condition of the version she signed reaching her (5.5.7, 5.10.6; draft 25's G10.6(b), gone); a power she never gave, the powers her version carries included, which its known parties gave and which bind only them (5.6.1(b), 5.10.7; "A filling gives no power", 27 November); a clock, a keeper or an anchoring she never accepted ending or ordering what she holds (5.10.8, 5.5.3, 5.13.5). What it does not give her: a say in updates, or any part of a version made after hers unless she signs it. She "can chose not to renew under new terms" (his words, 17:59).

**Left out where it reaches** (each listed in the brief, section 7, and in the trace): which act "the anchoring of the purchase" anchors (item 12); how long a version binds a filling whose words state no term (item 11); what a verifier shows of an agreement the known parties dissolved among themselves while her version still binds (item 13); whether words such as "renewed on the terms then current" are a later version reaching her (item 14; the settled note's M28, the lead's reading: "a buyer who signs them buys that knowingly; the grammar guarantees only that no update she did not sign reaches her"); the line's list "her rights, her spot, her place in the agreement" and "close the offer, or dissolve it among themselves" beyond his words (item 29).

---

## 4. Why the text is shaped so

**In plain words.** Draft 26 is short on purpose and says less than earlier drafts did. It warns that it is not to be used alone; it gives sockets, not answers; it names a keeper and says almost nothing more about it; it defines three verbs for what relays do. Each of those choices comes from something he said.

### 4.1 Only rules in the hashed text

"Only rules in the hashed text" (17 November): the MOR lead proposed taking everything that is not a rule out of the hashed text, "since a typo fixed in a history paragraph makes a new specification". He:

> "Yes, the text should be just that. Everything else goes into companion documents for the few who might be interested."

The lead's line: "a hashed text holds its rules and nothing else. The history, the open lists and the reasons go into companion documents beside it." So draft 25's quotations of his words, its *lie exposed / protects* lines, its history notes, its open lists and its stated costs are here, not in draft 26. Of old drafts he said, on 26 November: "Those old drafts should be buried deep, only for people who wish to do some archeology…" (section 11, M10's).

One thing in draft 26 is not a rule: its introduction (§0). "Agreements gets an introduction" (27 November) says "draft 26 opens with an introduction"; "a hashed text holds its rules and nothing else" stands against it. Whether the introduction is in the hashed text is the brief's item 15; draft 26 puts it there, as the assignment said (the outline, section 10, row 2).

### 4.2 The introduction's five points

All five rest on "Agreements gets an introduction" (27 November, 07:18–07:26), read whole. The MOR lead asked whether the warning should say plainly that Terms naming no keeper depend on every needed signer's knots. He:

> "I think agreement need an introduction that states a few things yes. I don’t think it should be stated as such."
>
> "Not to be used bare. Agreements offer the basic sockets to draft rule books, not one unified way of agreeing, more like a language used to decide how to agree."
>
> "Keepers (old “place”) gives the drafters of agreements a way to agree on where the agreement shall be stored. (This can be simply mentioned when introducing the concept of keeper)"
>
> "Knots is a MOR wide concept, but in agreement Rely is a good place to discuss their importance."

The lead's line: "draft 26 opens with an introduction that says: Agreements is not to be used bare; it offers the basic sockets to draft rule books, not one unified way of agreeing, more a language used to decide how to agree. The keeper is introduced as the drafters' way to agree where the agreement is kept. Knots are MOR-wide; in Agreements their importance is discussed where Rely is. The MOR lead's one-sentence warning about knots is not written as such. (The MOR lead's reading of his third line: "stored" means kept …)"

1. **Not to be used bare.** Draft 26 §0 opens: "**Agreements is not to be used bare.**", set in bold as the "big warning". His words, first in "No default way out" (17 November): "Yes, it.s still true under the right cMIPs. Agreements is not to be used bare. It's to build on top of."; then in "A late version 3 is rejected" (26 November, 15:37): "Probably right. This version of agreements is not to be used bare. Big warning needs to be placed on it." (the lead's line there: "Where and how the warning is written is for the rewrite"). The settled note's Q22 (the lead's): "The warning is the introduction's, not the verifier's output." Why (the scribe's reading): bare, the grammar has no default exit, no default buyer, no default for many sockets ("Named signers and buyer by assent stay cMIPs", 26 November); people agree through the cMIPs they name.
2. **Sockets for rule books; more a language.** Draft 26 §0: "It is not one unified way of agreeing. It offers the basic sockets from which rule books are drafted: more a language, used to decide how to agree, than a set of rules for agreeing. The rules people agree by are written in the cMIPs built on it." His words above, with 4.3 below. "than a set of rules for agreeing" is M2's wording (the trace, §0).
3. **The keeper, as the drafters' way to agree where it is kept.** Draft 26 says it twice, briefly: in §0 ("Among what it offers is the keeper: the way the drafters of an agreement agree on where the agreement is kept") and where the keeper is defined (§3.4, Keeper; 5.8.1), since his bracket asks for it "when introducing the concept of keeper" and the assignment put it in §0. "Kept" for his "stored" is the lead's reading. Then he (07:27): "Yes, stored like on any other relay, no?", which led to the three verbs (4.5).
4. **Knots are MOR-wide; their importance where Rely is.** Draft 26 §0: "Knots are a concept of all of MOR, not of Agreements alone. Their importance in agreements is taken up where this text treats relying on acts." Two readings of "where Rely is" (the outline, §0, item 4; neither chosen): (i) the rules on what an act names and relies on (5.13.1, 5.13.2: "An act names what it relied on"); (ii) relying as a witness act the client signs when a party signs (N10). N10 is not in draft 26 (section 5, N10), so in the text itself only (i) remains. Why knots matter is below, under 5.13.3.
5. **What it does not say as such.** His "I don’t think it should be stated as such" answers the MOR lead's sentence that Terms naming no keeper depend on every needed signer's knots: draft 26 does not write it. The fact stays in the rules: where Terms name no keeper, the pin is "by knots, where each party in whose name one of those assents is counted has made an act in that same name within the agreement that is shown after every one of those assents" (5.4.1), and a silent signer leaves the Terms not yet in force.

Not in the entry, so not in §0: the principle (section 3, by the 16:05 line); the three verbs and the relay market (4.5, 4.6).

### 4.3 The language, not the solution

The 15:38 addition, under "A late version 3 is rejected" (26 November). The entry, read whole: the MOR lead read a version 3 naming version 1 by the week's rules; he answered "Probably right. This version of agreements is not to be used bare. Big warning needs to be placed on it."; then, adding to that answer:

> "What I can state… when trying to build an agreement layer that could make everyone happy it became clear that it was best to treat it almost as a language and let the community implementations write the rules"

At 15:40 he said it answers the next question too ("Named signers and buyer by assent stay cMIPs"): "But now I see it adddresses the next question too, sorry". The lead's line there: "named signers and buyer by assent stay cMIPs, not grammar defaults. Agreements is treated almost as a language; the community's implementations write the rules."

It had been coming for ten days. "What the core is for" (17 November): "Those sentences are why agreement was not right… not because the ideas were wrong, but because it forces the protocol into a corner instead of doing what we meant. ID and text are universal, development is definition, the rest is to build on top of." "Between a protocol and a language" (17 November, recorded "for the companion and the paper"): "It's between a protocol and a language almost in some parts". "Built on top, as a test" (18 November): "Yes, agreement is now closer to language than protocol. It.s all about what is built on top." (the lead's line: "a rule that settles how people should behave in a deal belongs to the cMIPs built on top, not to the grammar").

What it does to the text (the scribe's reading): where an earlier draft settled how people behave (who may leave, when a delegation ends, what a receiver owes after an update), draft 26 has a socket and reads only its output. The update condition is the clearest: "It is one simple field, and these grammar rules read one thing of it: asked of a proposed update, **valid** or **not valid**" (5.5.7). The exceptions are where the grammar must hold whatever cMIP is plugged in: the principle (section 3, F16), the fork (5.12.1), a missing act (5.13.3).

**What is built on the blocks** (draft 25's §5.1, sent here by draft 26: the trace, §6). Draft 25 quoted him there: "Just be mindful to not attempt to solve something the cMIPs will." Five things people build; the grammar keeps the block under each. References are draft 26's; the scribe has marked the two rows whose block changed.

| What is built | The block the grammar keeps | Its cMIP plugs into |
|---|---|---|
| Paying through a sequence: a service that takes a work's payments and divides them | a receiver that may be an agreement (5.1.3, 5.1.4); an act that names the version it read (5.13.2); the two sides of a leg (5.11.1); the call and the leg report (5.11.2, 5.11.3; the block is the MICS's); the buyer's leg and its confirmation (5.11.7) | K6 (Control), the service being a hand; with K1 to read its deal, and a rule as a module to divide |
| The agreed witness: rewarding someone for being seen | a spot (§5.10); the `settlement` field (5.9.1) | K5 (Spot): the spot's cMIP names, as modules, the witness and the rule that counts |
| Closing, reopening, closing by itself: an offer that stops taking new people, or takes them again | a spot read from the version in force (5.10.2, 5.10.5); what a filling earned kept (5.10.6, 5.10.7) | K5 (Spot). *Changed block*: a filling is now kept from every update, not only its rights (5.10.6) |
| The update condition: which updates an agreement accepts, and signed by whom | one field, `condition`, of which the grammar reads only valid or not valid (5.5.7) | K4. *Changed block*: valid is the gate only; an update also needs the pin (5.5.1); the default signers are the known parties (5.5.5(a)) |
| Resolving a fork in advance | the resolving update (5.12.4); a power given in advance (§5.6, 5.12.5) | K3 (Power): the rule of the power to sign the resolving update, with a judge as a module where it names one |

Draft 25 added: escrow is built as the first row; a buyer's contest reaches the signers of what it is made against and not a service they picked (5.11.8); seeing beyond one leg is a data cMIP (K8, 5.11.5); and four things a cMIP could build were kept in the grammar (the named kinds of spot, the baker, the delegator's default, the counter signature). In draft 26 the delegator's default is gone ("Delegation is a leftover", 18 November) and the baker's rules are Envelopes' (5.11.9 keeps the contest). Two more stay by his words of draft 15, as draft 25 quotes them: the contest ("Making greed legible does not work without contest, it makes it core.") and the condition as one simple field ("It answers wether it can be updated or not. Because agreements make these conditions potentially complex the core still needs a simple field the cMIP can then specify in details.").

**What can be said in the language, and so is a cMIP's** (draft 25's §5.2). Draft 25 quoted him: "What the previous version of agreement focused on solving issues… this one supports and specifies the language for creating solutions" and "The answer is often: you did not use the right cMIP then." Each row was found by a review or a test and can be said as the language stands. References are draft 26's; rows a change of this stint reaches are marked by the scribe.

| What was found | How it can be said |
|---|---|
| Any two of three owners are to be enough to update the Terms | an update condition whose cMIP counts two signatures of three (5.5.7, K4); no power does it (5.6.2). *Now*: the update also needs its pin (5.5.1) |
| An owner's share is rewritten by an update fewer than all signed | an update condition that says which parts an update may change (5.5.7, K4) |
| A party wants to leave, and the Terms name no way | an update condition that answers valid for an update that party alone signed, where it takes its own signer out (5.5.7, K4) |
| A delegation only the agent may end | *No longer a row*: delegation is not a term ("Delegation is a leftover"); when a power ends is its own rule's (5.6.6(a), K3) |
| A group that co-owns a work is not counted when the work's Terms change | *Changed*: a party named as an agreement is a known party and among the default signers (5.5.5(a), the lead's reading, section 6); its coming into force still needs an `in_force` rule that counts it (5.4.4) |
| An outvoted member is seen from beyond their leg | the consent the data cMIP asks for (5.11.6, K8) |
| A contest reaches one agreement, and the money has already been passed on | escrow, as a service's cMIP (K6) |
| Nothing is shown to anyone before they sign | a body cMIP (K1), and the client |
| When and how a pool is divided | a service's cMIP (K6), with a rule as its module |
| A doer's report shows each side what every other was paid | the task's cMIP (K6) says which lines each side receives (5.11.3) |
| Someone receives under a call in Terms they did not sign | a cMIP closes it; draft 25 quotes him: "I’d use a data cMIP to bridge the gaps." (5.11.2) |
| An agreement no later version may change (a lock) | an update condition that answers not valid to every update (5.5.7(i), K4) |
| Those who may act for a group change who judges its disputes | not possible under a power (5.6.5(d)); only an update that passes the gate and the pin |
| The same work, baked by a copier; the baker sells her rights | *Envelopes' now*: the baker's rules moved there (G14); 5.11.9 keeps a baker's contest |
| A rival update signed late, against a version long since replaced | *Changed*: shown late by the knots, or by the keepers or the clock the version names, it replaces nothing (5.5.4); where nothing shows it, it and the replacing version are a fork (5.5.4, §5.12) |
| An agent signs, for a party, an update that changes the judge | the power's rule says how far the agent reaches (K3); the update still needs the gate and the pin (5.6.5(f)) |
| The one an agent acted for regrets what the agent signed | an update of the agreement, or, while one is available, a counter signature; and a version of the grant (5.6.6); nothing is set aside |

### 4.4 The layers split so relays can specialize

"Agreements gets an introduction", its addition of 07:37 (27 November). He:

> "Yes, the idea of MOR is to split the layers so relays can specialize. A relay that specializes in agreements wants to setup in a way where it can act as a keeper."

The lead's line: "the layers are split so that relays can specialize. A keeper is what a relay specialising in agreements sets itself up to be; the core names the role, and the relay market fills it."

So draft 26 defines a keeper by its role and by what a verifier takes from it, and nothing about how a relay becomes one: a keeper "relays the agreement's acts as any relay does, and vouches for their order only" (5.8.1). Why the grammar needs it at all: the strangers' "A shared sequence is needed for exactly two things: scarcity … and freshness" (section 1). Without a keeper, Terms get their order only from the signers' knots, and nothing proves that nothing later exists.

### 4.5 The three verbs, and why they are in §3.5

The same entry, 07:27. He asked: "Slight misunderstanding are always an opportunity to refine. Let9s decide once and for all the term used to describe what the relay does with the acts?" The texts disagreed (Envelopes: "store and pass on", "carry, fetch and mirror"; Identity: "store"). He: "Dunno why, I’d like to fine a one word term… even if we have to invent it"; liked "shelves", then: "Wait… it also means to put an idea away… the truth is the a relay relays". Then:

> "Agreed. An uploader lodges, the relay relays, and the client fetches."

The lead's line: "three verbs, one per actor: an uploader **lodges** an act at a relay; the relay **relays** it (takes it in, passes it on, reads nothing, vouches for nothing); a client **fetches** it. A keeper is a relay that relays the agreement's acts and adds the order. "Store", "carry" and "mirror" for what a relay does give way to these when the texts are next touched".

**Why §3.5, and why "MOR's"** (the lead's reading on the outline's rows, as the trace has it: "defined in §3.5 for this text's use and marked as MOR-wide, for Envelopes to carry"). The verbs belong to every layer ("give way to these when the texts are next touched"), and relays are defined in Envelopes. Two readings were open (the outline, section 10, row 6): define them in draft 26, where the exchange was had, or below it and use them here. Draft 26 does the first and says so: "These three verbs are MOR's, for every layer, one for each actor; this text uses them as defined here." Envelopes takes them when it is next written (M10's "What draft 26 hands to other texts"). "Uploader" is his word, wider than Envelopes draft 12's (a bake's signer); draft 26 uses it as he said it (the lead's reading of 09:16, section 6).

### 4.6 The keeper, "that and nothing more"

"The place vouches for the chaining only" (26 November, 17:47–17:49). He asked to be reminded of the place: "Remind me “place” definition pls". Then:

> "So “place” is the “official relay for the agreement” it tracks the chain and vouch for it, redundancy can also live elsewhere"
>
> "Yes, vouch for the chaining only"
>
> "Two disagreeing has its value though"
>
> "Yes, they should. Can we find a better name for “place”?"
>
> "Why not use keeper again?"

The lead's lines: "the place is the agreement's official relay. It tracks the agreement's chain and vouches for the chaining only (which act came after which), never for an act's validity. Copies of the acts can live elsewhere for redundancy; only the named place's order counts"; "Terms may name more than one place … where they disagree there is none ("not shown", with that reason), and the disagreement is kept as evidence"; and ""place" is renamed **keeper**: the agreement's official relay, named in its Terms, vouching for the chaining only, defined in draft 26 as that and nothing more; the same word as Identity's."

Why "nothing more": the MOR lead had set "keeper" aside on 17 November ("The place is a pointer": he, "So, it's a keeper of sorts?", then "It's just a pointer to a relay then. Why the need for more grammar?"; the lead's line: "the place is a pointer to a relay, a field in Terms, with one rule: where the Terms name a place, every reader takes what is latest from it and from nothing else"). Older drafts had keeper machinery. Taking the word back was safe only if draft 26 defines the keeper as the pointer and its one rule. So draft 26's keeper is a relay the Terms name; it vouches for order and never for validity (5.8.1); copies anywhere count for nothing (5.8.2); several keepers count where they agree (5.8.3); a keeper's record gives "for each act, its id and its position" (§3.4, the lead's reading of 09:16).

Left out where "nothing more" meets a choice (the brief, items 5 and 6): with several keepers, whether the pin needs every keeper or those that show it where none disagrees; and what a keeper answers beyond order (draft 25's "what is whole, who holds what, whether a period has passed, or whether something was made public"): (i) nothing, on "that and nothing more"; (ii) a period passed, on the 27 November line "Terms state their anchors (a keeper, a clock to anchor to, or both)". Draft 26 writes 5.8.4 "for order only" and says "A keeper is not a clock" (5.8.6, the lead's reading of 10:07).

---

## 5. The reasoning, rule by rule

**In plain words.** For every rule draft 26 changed or added, this section says which exchange made it, quotes his words, and labels the MOR lead's line. Rules that stayed as draft 25 had them are not repeated (section 9, M10's, traces every rule). Draft 25 closed each of its fourteen groups of rules with two lines, *lie exposed* and *protects*; they come here, under the draft 26 section that holds the group, kept where they still hold, rewritten where the rule changed, and marked so.

**The two questions.** Draft 25's §1 ended: "Every grammar rule here answers two questions: which lie does it expose that signed, ordered text cannot already expose, and whom does it protect who did not choose it." Draft 26 leaves the sentence out, as reasoning ("Only rules in the hashed text", 17 November; the trace, §1). It came from Fable's review of 10 November: "First: is there a lie here that signed, ordered text cannot already expose? If not, it is only text. Second: does catching it hurt someone who did not choose the rule? If not, it is a task for a cMIP." (`docs/reviews/fable-after-the-task-list.md`, Fable's proposal there). The *lie exposed* and *protects* lines below are each rule group's answers to the two questions.

**How each entry reads.** *Draft 25*: the rule it was, and what the brief sorted it. *What changed*: in a line. *The exchange*: heading and date, his words, and the lead's line where it says more. Where a change rests on a reading of the MOR lead's of 27 November, the entry says so and section 6 gives the reading in full. An entry marked *new* has no draft 25 rule; the brief's numbers N1 to N10 are given.

### Wave five: his three decisions of 27 November, evening

*Added by P2, the scribe's second session of wave five. Each decision with his words quoted whole from its exchange, the MOR lead's turns and lines labelled, and the rules each shaped (P1's "by rule" lists, the trace). Where the lead's lines say more than his words, it is said.*

**1. A filling gives no power** (27 November, 18:39–18:47; Fable 5.1's fifth attack, pull request 306, its first break). *The case*, the lead's retelling in the ledger: "a seller writes into the version Ana buys a power of kind `assent` for the seller over every filler; Ana's assent is her signature on that version, so by "a power counts only if that person signed the version that gives it" (18 November) the seller assents for her to version 3, and it reaches her by 5.10.7's "a power she gave"". The MOR lead leaned one rule: "no power a version carries can be used for a filling of that version; only her own signature, or an agent's under a power she gave in her own name". He: "Yup, this should not happen. Not sure I caught all the details though" The MOR lead retold it plainly. He: "Buona did they build it in agreements?" The MOR lead: yes, as the text stood, rule by rule; "the hole is the word "gave": Ana accepted an offer, the known parties wrote the version and its powers". He: "Unknown party, different rule, what do you think? The rule used is meant for known parties" *The lead's line*: "the rule "a power counts only if that person signed the version that gives it" is for known parties, who write and sign versions. An unknown party's assent is an acceptance, not a signing of the version: it gives no power to anyone. A power can be used for a filling only where the filling's holder granted it herself, in an act of her own." *The lead's sentence for draft 26*, to check: "A power a version carries is given by its known parties and binds only them. An assent that fills a spot gives no power. An act made for a filling's holder under a power counts as her signature only where she gave that power in an act of her own." He corrected it (18:46): "“that fills an unknown party spot”" *The lead's line*: "the sentence reads "An assent that fills an unknown party spot gives no power." A spot a known party fills is a known party's act, and the known parties' rule applies to it." *Why*: his "The rule used is meant for known parties": a power is given by those who write and sign a version; she accepted an offer and wrote nothing. *Shaped*: §3.2 **Power** (P1-7), 5.6.1(b) (P1-29), 5.6.3 (P1-30), 5.10.6 (P1-41), 5.10.7 (P1-42); with his correction, §3.2 **Unknown party** (P1-6), 5.10.6 (P1-40), 5.4.3, 5.6.1(c), 5.6.2 (P1-55 to 57). *Beyond his words* (the lead's): "binds only them"; "in an act of her own" (his words say whose rule it is, not which act gives a power: the lead's reading 5 of 20:41, section 6.16); "the known parties' rule applies to it" (a known party's own filling: the lead's reading 7, section 6.16). Scenarios 8.27, 8.29, 8.34, 8.36.

**2. Two classes** (27 November, 18:47, in the same exchange). He: "It’s seems important to really make a point that these two are not the same class or IDs and are not bound by the same rules." *The lead's line*: "known parties and unknown parties are two classes of participant in an agreement, not two situations of one class. Each has its own rules: the known parties write, sign, update and give powers, and are bound by the versions they sign; an unknown party fills a spot, is bound by the version she accepted, gives no power, has no say in updates, and no update reaches her. Draft 26 says so as a point of its own, in the introduction and in the definitions, not only rule by rule." *Why*: the hole of the first decision came from reading one rule over both classes. *Shaped*: §0 (P1-1, two sentences, no rule), §3.2 **Two classes** (P1-3, a paragraph before any entry). *Beyond his words* (the lead's): the list of each class's rules; the lead's reading 12 of 20:55 says each item is already a grammar rule (5.6.1(b), 5.6.1(c), §5.10) and that P3 cites the rule beside each (section 6.16). Where the principle is written: the lead's reading F18 and his reading 11 of 20:55, both: one sentence in §0, the reasons here (section 3).

**3. A dead keeper: an update every needed signer signs needs no keeper** (27 November, 18:44–18:51; Fable 5.1's fifth attack, break 3: "an update is pinned per the keepers of the version it replaces, so a keeper that stops answering freezes the agreement for good, and "both keepers are needed" doubles the lock", the lead's retelling). The MOR lead leaned that leaving a keeper follows the agreement: "the Terms say what happens when it falls silent, and Terms that say nothing accept the lock". He: "A dead keeper forces a new version of the agreement, no?" The MOR lead: "yes, but as draft 26 stands that version is pinned by the dead keeper and never pinned; the hatch: an update that replaces the keepers may be pinned by the knots of every needed signer". He: "The agreement needs the keeper to run. Does it need the keeper for an update with new signatures?" The MOR lead: "no; a keeper orders what the parties do not all sign (a withdrawal against a completion, a majority's update, a buyer's assent, a late version); an update every needed signer signs, each after the other, carries its own order and is pinned by their knots whatever keeper is named; signed by fewer, it is pinned by every keeper named, or not shown; a dead keeper costs the known parties one thing, all signing the version that replaces it." He: "Sounds good" *The lead's line*: "the pin splits in two. An update signed by every signer it needs, each shown after the other, is pinned by their knots, whether or not the Terms name a keeper. An update signed by fewer than all it needs is pinned by every keeper the Terms name, or is not shown. A dead keeper is left by a new version that every needed signer signs. A filling's proof stands as the keeper of her version showed it. The MOR lead's "leaving a keeper follows the agreement" and "Terms that say nothing accept the lock" are withdrawn." *Why*: his "The agreement needs the keeper to run": a keeper orders what not everyone signs; an update everyone it needs signs carries its own order. *Shaped*: 5.5.1(b) (P1-20, P1-59), §3.3 **Pin** (P1-8), 5.4.1's pointer (P1-18), 5.5.5(d) (P1-28, P1-58), 5.5.3 (P1-60), 5.8.3 (P1-35), 5.8.4 (P1-36, P1-37), 5.10.2 (P1-39), §1 (P1-2, the reading F19). *Beyond his words* (the lead's): "each shown after the other" (the lead's turn and line; the lead's reading 1 of 20:41 reads it: "every needed signer's act is shown after every needed assent"); which signers an update "needs" (reading 1: "those the condition's cMIP requires"; the lead's turn counts "a majority's update" among what a keeper orders: the two are set side by side in scenarios 8.4 and 8.30); "A filling's proof stands as the keeper of her version showed it"; a first version (reading 2, scenario 8.31); lateness with keepers named (reading 3, scenario 8.32). Scenario 8.28.

**And after P1: Publishing the Terms is the publisher's assent** (27 November, 20:49–20:50; Fable 5.1's fifth attack, its question 1.1; the lead's reading 4 of 20:41). The MOR lead asked whether the act that publishes the Terms counts as its signer's assent, leaning yes. He: "What is number 4?" The MOR lead retold it. He: "Yes, I don’t see the need to have her sign twice" *The lead's line*: "the act that publishes the Terms is its signer's assent to them; the signer does not sign again. The other parties assent later, each in an act of their own." Draft 26 as P1 left it does not yet say it (§4.3, **An author's first act**; 5.4.1; 5.5.7(e)): its words are P3's (scenario 8.33; section 6.16).

### §4.1 The set of acts (N3, new)

*What it is*: one value form for naming a set of acts: the ids sorted, repeats dropped, hashed with a label; "It says nothing of whether the set is complete, what it means, or when it was read" (§3.1). *The exchange*: "One way to name a set of acts, in Agreements" (26 November, 16:11–16:16). Three honest readers had turned one list of acts into different fingerprints, no rule saying how a set is written as bytes. He: "You are going to expand what the set of byte is and what it does pls"; "Should it be in envelopes though? It feels like agreements stuff"; "Knots making is core core business, a" (read as option a, the grammar's own value form). *The lead's line*: "Agreements' grammar gets one byte form for naming a set of acts … so that Terms and every cMIP built on Agreements name the same set the same way." *Readings*: the empty set and "no knot by itself" are the settled note's M21 (the ledger silent, item 23); which fields take it is the lead's reading of 09:27, narrowed at 09:47 (section 6): `resolves` keeps the list of the tips' ids.

### §5.1 An agreement as a party

*Draft 25's lines* (G1), still holding: *Lie exposed*: ""I receive for that work", said by someone who does not, by that work's own Terms; and "the work agreed", said of a signing its own Terms gave no one the power to make." *Protects*: "The parties of an agreement that is pointed at, who are not bound by what they did not sign; the parties of one that signs, who are bound only as their own Terms say; and the payer, who needs to know only the work."

**5.1.2** (G1.2, changes). *What changed*: an agreement's signature binds a party of it "only where that party's own signature is on the version of x's Terms that gives the power: no update condition can give it for a party who did not sign that version"; "a named delegate" becomes "a signer its Terms give a power to sign for it". *The exchanges*: "Nothing about me without my signature" (18 November). He: "Absolutely, no need to even debate this. Nothing about me is accepted without my signature. But, I suspect we opened Pandora's box… how to fire an employee?" The lead's line: "a power to act for someone counts only where that person's own signature is on the version that gives it. No update condition can give it for her." And "Delegation is a leftover" (18 November). He: "I think it's best. It's a leftover of the old ways". The lead's line: "delegation is not a concept of its own. Acting for someone is Terms with a power; when the power ends is for the power's own rule, the cMIP it names, to answer."

**5.1.4** (G1.4, two ways in the brief; changes on the lead's reading). *What changed*: a receiver taken from Terms in force is "per the keepers that agreement's Terms name, qualified **per keeper** … or, where they name none, **as shown**"; draft 25's "per the anchoring the reader accepts" goes. *The exchange*: "Whose place counts; protect the unknown party, as a principle" (26 November, section 3). He: "I think you are right. It is worth turning it into a principle. Protect the unknown party". The reading that settled it is the lead's (section 6, the held rules: G1.4).

**Wave five, the readings carried** (P1's edits, and P2's in encoding 3; every row the MOR lead's, `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-draft-26-readings.md`, in full in section 6.14). One line for each row, under the rule it shaped first:

- **C6 (the version a payment names)**: "Any version shown as a version of that agreement; whether a filling stood under it is 5.10's." *Shaped*: 5.1.4 (P1-14).

### §5.2 The words, and what the Terms use

*Draft 25's lines* (G2), still holding: *Lie exposed*: ""The document we referred to said something else", and "there was a side piece"." *Protects*: "Every reader who arrives later, and the party who signed without fetching what was referred to." With the words now a Text act (5.2.2), a reader who may read them can fetch and check them by their own act id (the scribe's addition).

**5.2.2** (N4, new). *What it says*: the words of Terms are a Text act, public or private, which the Terms name in `body` by its act id; it comes first; where the act `body` names is held and is not a Text act, the Terms are invalid. *The exchange*: "The words of Terms are a Text act" (26 November, 16:25–16:28). The MOR lead set out two ways: one act, the words loose bytes named by a fingerprint; or two acts, the words published as a Text act and named by its act id, "the cost one more act per agreement". He: "How many acts are involved here?", then "I think it”s cleaner, it keeps text to text and agreement grammar to agreement grammar, both have their own hashes to verify", and (16:28) "Text comes first as a rule since agreement needs to point to it". *The lead's lines*: "the words of Terms are an act of the Text layer, public or private, and the Terms name it by its act id … The hash choice for loose words goes"; "Terms can name only an act that already exists, so the words are published before the Terms that point to them." *Readings*: "naming it by its act id is all that this asks of them" is the settled note's Q25; the invalid case (a held act that is not a Text act) is the lead's reading of 09:27 (section 6).

(5.2.3 stays as G2.2, with one entry added to what is left unread: the answer of `in_force` on coming into force, in an update. That is the settled note's Q7, narrowed by the lead's reading of 09:47, row 3: section 6.)

**Wave five, the readings carried** (P1's edits, and P2's in encoding 3; every row the MOR lead's, `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-draft-26-readings.md`, in full in section 6.14). One line for each row, under the rule it shaped first:

- **F11 (words disowned by a rotation; a Text act invalid under Text's rule)**: "Terms whose words act is void are unchecked as to their words, the Terms standing; parties' clients relied on the words, so the case is rare; a Text act invalid under its own layer is no Text act for 5.2.2." *Shaped*: 5.2.2 (P1-15).
- **C16, F11 (the act `body` names, held and unreadable, or not held)**: "Unchecked, with a note of what is lacking; several acts checked together, the note names each." *Shaped*: 5.2.2 (P1-15); encoding 3, E25 (P2).

### §5.3 Authors

*Draft 25's lines* (G3), still holding: *Lie exposed*: ""We wrote this together", or "I meant something else by that word", said by the one who wrote it." *Protects*: "The party who accepted words they did not write." The ground is the strangers' "assign in advance who bears the failure" (section 1).

**5.3.1** (G3.1, two ways in the brief; changes on the lead's reading). *What changed*: an author is named as a signer, "never a party named by role, and never one named as an agreement". *The exchange*: "There is no unsigned act: the author is a known party" (27 November, 07:13–07:18). He: "Let’s speak Mor…. terms are record, agreement is law, right? There is no such thing as an unsigned act on Mor", then "Sounds right." *The lead's line*: "Terms are a record (a signed act); the agreement is the law (what is in force). No act is unsigned, so no Terms are nobody's: the author is a known party". Why it changes: draft 25 let `authors` name a role, and a role is never a known party; the lead's reading of 09:27 narrows it to a signer (section 6).

**5.3.1, its signer** (wave four, N1; with §3.2 **Author**, §4.2 `authors`, §4.4 case 5, and encoding 3's E13). *What changed* (N1's words, corrected in wave five, P1-16): "The signer of a Terms act is its first author, whether or not `authors` names them: `authors` names the other authors, and MAY name the signer too. No Terms are invalid for leaving their signer out of `authors`. The other authors sign by assents of their own, made after the Terms act". N1 had written: "`authors` MUST name, as a party named as a signer, the signer of the Terms act that carries them, and Terms whose `authors` do not name their own signer are **invalid**". *The exchange*: "The signer of a Terms act is an author" (27 November, 16:48–16:50), the first of the two questions carried to the end of stage two (the scribe's M3). The MOR lead asked whether the signer of a Terms act must be among its authors, leaning yes, by "no unsigned act". He: "Absolutely. The person publishing the act is by default an author, the rest adds signature later". *The lead's line*, as the ledger now has it: "the signer of a Terms act is an author of it, by default: an author whether or not `authors` lists them, so `authors` names the other authors, and such Terms are valid. The other authors add their signatures afterwards, each by an assent of their own. (Corrected on 27 November, evening: the MOR lead's first line here, "a Terms act whose `authors` leaves its signer out is invalid", overreached his "by default"; caught by the scribe and the codemeister.)" The lead's reading of 18:41: ""by default" means without needing to be listed" (section 6.15). Why: Terms are a record, signed ("There is no such thing as an unsigned act on Mor", 07:13); the one who signs the record is one of those whose words it is, and the others' signatures come after, as assents (§4.3, **An author's first act**). N1 wrote "invalid" on the lead's first line; wave five follows the corrected line (P1-4, P1-12, P1-13, P1-16; encoding 3's E13). His 20:50 answer ("Publishing the Terms is the publisher's assent", the ledger) goes one step further: the Terms act is its signer's assent; draft 26's words for it are P3's (section 6.16, reading 4). A resolving update carries no `authors` (§4.3) and is not asked it. The question closes the first carried question and the found item on the double assent's author where it turned on it (the report).

**Wave five, the readings carried** (P1's edits, and P2's in encoding 3; every row the MOR lead's, `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-draft-26-readings.md`, in full in section 6.14). One line for each row, under the rule it shaped first:

- **C1, S (the signer among the authors)**: "The signer is the first author whether or not listed; `authors` names the others; no Terms are invalid for leaving the signer out." *Shaped*: 5.3.1 (P1-16), §3.2 **Author** (P1-4), §4.2 `authors` (P1-12), §4.4 case 5 (P1-13); encoding 3, E13 and section 9, step 7.4 (P2).

### §5.4 The first version, and what an assent binds

**5.4.1** (G4.1, two ways in the brief; changes on the lead's ruling on item 1). *What changed*: a first version comes into force by its assents, as before, and is in force only once their order is shown: by the keepers it names, or, where it names none, by each party whose assent counted acting again in the agreement, shown after every completing assent. Any such act pins, a counter signature included. *The exchange*: "In force once pinned" (18 November). Fable had found that a withdrawal signed a year later could not be told from one signed in time; the MOR lead leaned that Terms count as in force only once their order is pinned, "one more round of signatures for a bare agreement, and no signature taken back after it". He: "Agreed. Let them get stuck and cook it clean" *The lead's line*: "Terms are in force only once the order of the signatures that put them in force is pinned: a named place shows it, or each signer whose signature was needed has acted after the completion, citing it. Until then they are "not yet in force". A withdrawal after the pinning changes nothing." Why it was two ways: on 26 November (16:19) the lead's line said "The first version comes into force by the assents of its named parties", and no later line said whether the pin still waits. The ruling on item 1 and the 09:47 reading on M4's row 2 are in section 6.

**5.4.3** (G4.3, changes). *What changed*: an assent binds only while its Terms are in force, "with one exception: an assent by which its signer earned an unknown party's filling (§3.2) binds that signer, and binds the known parties to them, as the version it names says, for as long as that version's words say, on the clock that version names, whether or not a later version is in force; no update reaches it". An assent that earned a filling under a replaced version is shown, with that version named (the settled note's M15). *The exchanges*: "For the unknown party there is no update, only the agreement she signed" (27 November) and "A buyer under a replaced version" (26 November, 15:52): "Ana acted when version 2 was in force. She is now bound by the agreement layer and so is the seller." (Section 3.) "Binds the known parties to them" is the lead's line's "binds the seller to her" in the text's words; where the known parties are not all sellers the two may differ (the trace, M4's rows to check, 7).

**5.4.5** (G4.10, changes). *What changed*: a counter signature is available until the completing assents are shown before it; one shown after them is late, cancels nothing, and pins like any other act; a counter signature and a completion that nothing orders are a fork: neither takes effect, and the Terms are not in force until it is resolved. Draft 25 answered that case "not shown". *The exchanges*: "What a race stops" (18 November): Ben completes Terms while Carla takes back her signature, nothing ordering the two. He: "Agreed, Carla is the monkey wrench that gets it all stuck". The lead's line: "in a race, the change of state fails and the earlier state stays". Then "A fork is a fork" (27 November, 07:01–07:08), which puts that race under the word fork: "Neither takes effect; the earlier state stays" (the lead's line). Where the window closes is the lead's reading of 09:47 and 10:07 (section 6).

*The found item, answered for the companion*: once that fork is resolved, does Carla's counter signature cancel? The lead's reading (09:47): "as the resolver decides; the 18 November "withdrawal stands" and the 27 November "neither takes effect" agree (not in force, she is not bound)". While the fork stands the Terms are not in force (5.4.5); how a fork that is not of a line is resolved in bytes is left out (item 7; 5.12.4 ends "These grammar rules give the form of a resolving update for a fork of a line only").

**Wave five, the readings carried** (P1's edits, and P2's in encoding 3; every row the MOR lead's, `docs/lead/notes/2026-11-27-draft-26-readings.md`, in full in section 6.14). One line for each row, under the rule it shaped first:

- **C2 (which assents the pin reaches, with an `in_force` cMIP)**: "Only the assents the agreement's own rule requires; the cMIP in `in_force` names which (K2's output). Not every assent counted." *Shaped*: 5.4.1 (P1-17), §6 K2 (P1-52).
- **C20 (which acts pin a party's side)**: "A Terms act or Statement of that agreement, in that name. The pin is the party acting within the agreement after completion, not any knot anywhere." *Shaped*: 5.4.1 (P1-17).
- **C5, F4's phantom `previous`**: "An act named and not held leaves the pin unchecked wherever it could be a needed party's later act." *Shaped*: 5.4.1 (P1-18).
- **C15 (two counter signatures in one name that nothing orders)**: "By knots or keepers only; where nothing orders them, not by id: both stand as one withdrawal." *Shaped*: 5.4.5 (P1-19).

### §5.5 Updates: the gate and the pin

*Draft 25's lines* (G4, for §5.4 and §5.5), rewritten here, the scribe's, where the rules changed. *Lie exposed* (draft 25, holding): ""You signed, so you are bound", said by a party who never signed; "these are the Terms now", said of Terms replaced, of one branch of two, of a replacement that lacks a signature, or of one that the agreement's own update condition answers not valid; "we were first", said of an update the acts or the place show was made after the version it names was replaced; and "this agreement is over"." *Rewritten*: "these are the Terms now" is exposed also where an update is valid and its order not shown (5.5.1); "we were first" is shown by the knots, or by the keepers or the clock the version names, and by no anchoring the Terms do not name (5.5.4(b)); and "this agreement is over" is exposed as an update like any other (5.5.6). *Protects* (draft 25): "The first to sign; the party left out of a replacement; everyone in the versions made since, whom a late update cannot take back to an old one where the acts or the place show it late; everyone in the last version, which keeps binding until an update its own update condition answers valid replaces it; and whoever relies on a part of an agreement that its update condition lets no update touch." *Rewritten*: "until an update passes its gate and its pin replaces it"; "where the acts, the keepers or the clock show it late"; and, added, the unknown party, whom no update reaches (5.10.6), and the known parties, none of whom is bypassed by a default that counts fillers (5.5.5(a)).

**5.5.1** (N1, new). *What it says*: an update replaces the version it names only where it passes two checks: **the gate**, that version's update condition (or its default) answers valid; and **the pin**, its order is shown, by the keepers that version names or, where it names none, by knots. *The exchange*: "The gate and the pin; a fork, not a race" (26 November, 17:39–17:46), refining "Valid and in force, two words, two jobs" (18:00) and "Valid means in force: one gate for an update" (16:19). At 16:19 he had said "I agree with CP. to me it means the same thing… valid, in force, what’s the difference for the core? The core should only see one version as valid", and the lead's line made one gate, two valid updates being "a race". At 17:39 the MOR lead asked whether the pin stays, being "order and not consent". He: "I think I got it but want to be sure. Reword it pls"; "So the pin and the gates are…"; "In MOR terms, in specs pls"; then "How is this then enforced? Client compliance? When I think about agreements, I always think about malicious clients…"; "The agree,ent own way of resolving forks… how is that decided?"; and "Ok, that”s what I was trying to remember we forced the deadlock and push them to resolve it". *The lead's line*: "an update passes two checks: the gate … and the pin … Two updates that both pass the gate and that nothing orders are a fork … The deadlock is forced on purpose, to push them to resolve it. "Race", the MOR lead's word on 26 November, is withdrawn for updates of Terms." The wording of G4.8 and G4.7 in the entry is the MOR lead's (item 31, section 6). Why: one gate alone let two valid updates of one version both stand, or neither, with no way out; the pin turns that into a fork, which the agreement's own people must resolve. On malicious clients the lead answered there: "by verification, every honest verifier computing the same answer from signed bytes"; a malicious client "can sign a rival (a visible fork), withhold its knot (the silent signer: not shown, cured by naming a place), withhold acts (unchecked, never a yes), lie to its own user (client conformance, cured by any other client); a lying place's lie is signed."

**5.5.2** (G4.5, changes). *What changed*: Terms stop when an update that replaces them is in force, by both checks; draft 25 read the replacing Terms' "in force" by its own first-version rule. *The exchanges*: "The gate and the pin" and "Valid and in force" (26 November). He (18:00): "Agreed." The lead's line: ""valid" answers the gate only … "In force" answers gate and pin". The sentence that replaced Terms "may still govern a filling" is "Who signs an update by default" (17:59): "If it’s already there… all good".

**5.5.3** (G4.6, changes). *What changed*: which Terms are in force is taken "per the keepers the Terms name, qualified **per keeper** … or, where they name none, from the knots, **as shown**"; a keeper or anchoring of the reader's own choosing "never gives the Terms' own answer, never puts Terms in force, and never makes an update late". *The exchanges*: "The place vouches for the chaining only" (26 November): "Yes, vouch for the chaining only"; "Whose place counts" (26 November, section 3). The settled note's M20; the lead's reading on G1.4, reaching this rule (section 6).

**5.5.4** (G4.7, changes). *What changed*: two Terms acts that name the same version, each passing its gate, neither shown after the other, are a fork; draft 25's test "each has the assents its own `in_force` requires" becomes the gate. A **late update** is one shown after the acts that put the replacing version through the gate and the pin (the lead's reading of 09:47, "Late update, one test"). *The exchanges*: "The gate and the pin" (26 November); "A fork is a fork" (27 November): "A fork is a fork." And "A late version 3 is rejected" (26 November, 15:33–15:37): "Version 3 is the one the network needs to reject". The answer word is **late**, not "rejected": "Rejected" is not an answer word in the settled note's M5, the lead's reading; whether "rejected" is a verifier's answer is left out (item 24).

**5.5.4(a)** (G4.7(a), changes). *What changed*: "Every fingerprint in a field of every act the verifier holds is a knot, Terms or Statement, of this agreement or another, of any layer"; where O names keepers, knots show L late only as a named keeper's contradiction. *The exchange*: "Knots are knots: one reading, "shown after"" (27 November, 07:38–07:49), under 5.13.3. The keepers' clause is the lead's reading of 10:07 (section 6).
